========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, doctor. Thank you so much for fitting me in. Um... I'm a little embarrassed to even bring this up, to be honest. I hope it's okay to talk about this here. 

I'm a high school teacher, and... well, lately I've had to watch back some of my lesson videos. And I just can't stop seeing this crooked, sort of swollen-looking side of my face on the recordings. I know it probably sounds silly, or like I'm making a big deal out of nothing, but once I notice it, I just... I feel like the students, and their parents, and the rest of the staff must be staring right at it instead of listening to anything I'm actually teaching. 

I hope that makes sense? I just want to make sure I'm doing my job well, but it's just been really distracting me lately.

Um... well, it really is mostly just that one side of my face. The unevenness. I mean, I guess I'm just so focused on it because... well, I can't stop seeing how crooked and swollen it looks on those lesson videos I have to record. 

I really hate to admit this, because I know it sounds so vain, and I promise I'm usually very focused on the actual teaching... but once I notice it on the screen, I just get this overwhelming feeling that everyone—the students, the parents, the other staff—they must all just be staring at my face instead of listening to a single word I'm saying. 

I don't want you to think I'm self-obsessed or anything. I just want to be a good teacher, and it's hard when I feel like this huge flaw is distracting everyone. I hope that doesn't make me sound terrible... is that the kind of information you were looking for?

Um... well... I guess I think about it a lot. Especially lately. 

I know I've mentioned the lesson videos a couple of times already, and I really hope I don't sound like a broken record... I just want to make sure I'm explaining myself right. Um... wow, that's... that's a really good question. I, um... 

I want to say yes? I really want to say that if everyone told me it was fine, I'd just believe them and move on. I mean, I'd certainly appreciate them saying it, and I'd probably smile and thank them so I wouldn't seem ungrateful or... or argumentative. I hate being difficult. 

But... if I'm being completely honest—and I really am trying to be as honest as I can with you—I don't know if it would actually change what I see in the mirror. Or on the screen. Because when I look at the videos, it's just so... obvious to me. The unevenness is right there. So even if everyone was being incredibly kind and telling me I looked normal, I think a part of me would just assume they were... well, just being nice. Trying to make me feel better, you know? Because people are good like that. 

I hope that doesn't make me sound stubborn or... or like I wouldn't trust people. I really do value other people's opinions! It's just... I see what I see, and it's hard to un-see it, even if I want to. Does that make sense? I hope that's not a frustrating answer.

Um... well... I guess I'd have to say yes, it is. It's definitely making things harder than they used to be. 

I really pride myself on being a good teacher, and I love being involved at the school. But lately... um... I've just been falling behind on things. I know I shouldn't let something like my face get in the way of my career, and I'm really trying to push past it, but it's just so overwhelming. 

I hope you don't think less of me for letting it affect my job. I really am trying my best. Is it... is it normal for something like this to mess up your work life so much?

I mean, I guess I'm just so focused on it because... well, I can't stop seeing how crooked and swollen it looks on those lesson videos I have to record. \n\nI really hate to admit this, because I know it sounds so vain, and I promise I'm usually very focused on the actual teaching... but once I notice it on the screen, I just get this overwhelming feeling that everyone—the students, the parents, the other staff—they must all just be staring at my face instead of listening to a single word I'm saying. \n\nI don't want you to think I'm self-obsessed or anything. I just want to be a good teacher, and it's hard when I feel like this huge flaw is distracting everyone. I hope that doesn't make me sound terrible... is that the kind of information you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: Do you think that others actually notice this unevenness on your face, or is it just your own perception?\n\nPatient: Um... well, I know I just sort of mentioned this a second ago, but... because I just can't stop seeing how crooked and swollen it looks on those lesson videos, I... I really do believe they notice it. \n\nI don't want to sound paranoid or anything, I promise I'm usually a very rational person. I know you might think it's just my own perception, and I really wish it was... but yes, I feel like the students, the parents, the staff... they must all be seeing it and staring at it instead of listening to my lessons. I know they're probably too polite to point it out to my face, but it just feels so obvious to me that they're looking right at it. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm crazy or delusional for feeling that way... I really try to be objective about things. And the students, well, they haven't said it directly to me, anyway. I'm sure they probably just talk about it behind my back instead of being rude to my face. \n\nBut no, no direct comments or feedback confirming it. I hope... I hope that makes sense? I'm really trying to be as honest as I can, I just don't want you to think I'm wasting your time if no one else has pointed it out. I mean, my family and friends are all wonderful, really supportive people. I... So even if everyone was being incredibly kind and telling me I looked normal, I think a part of me would just assume they were... well, just being nice. Trying to make me feel better, you know? Because people are good like that. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound stubborn or... or like I wouldn't trust people. I really do value other people's opinions! It's just... I see what I see, and it's hard to un-see it, even if I want to. Does that make sense? I hope that's not a frustrating answer.\n\nDoctor: Has the specific feature of your face that you're concerned about, the unevenness, changed at all since you first started noticing it?\n\nPatient: Um... well, no, I don't think it's actually changed. I mean, physically, it's... it's the same. \n\nI know I've been talking about it a lot, and I really don't want to give the impression that it's, like, growing or mutating or anything crazy like that. I'm not delusional, I promise! It's just... the unevenness has always been there, I guess, but I just didn't really focus on it until I had to watch myself teach on those videos. \n\nSo the feature itself hasn't changed... maybe just how much I notice it? I hope that's an okay answer. I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right information so I'm not wasting your time. Does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concern about the unevenness on your face is interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Um... well... I guess I'd have to say yes, it is. It's definitely making things harder than they used to be. \n\nI really pride myself on being a good teacher, and I love being involved at the school. But lately... um... I've just been falling behind on things. Like, my lesson planning isn't getting done, and I haven't been as present for mentoring the younger students. Even with coaching the basketball team, I'm just... I'm missing things. \n\nI spend so much time worrying about how I look, or trying to fix it, that my work just isn't getting finished on time anymore. And... I guess it's gotten bad enough that the administrators have noticed. They've started documenting my missed deadlines and some issues with coverage. \n\nI'm so ashamed to admit that, honestly. I've always been the reliable one, you know? And now I'm genuinely terrified I might lose my position if I can't get this under control. I know I shouldn't let something like my face get in the way of my career, and I'm really trying to push past it, but it's just so overwhelming. \n\nI hope you don't think less of me for letting it affect my job. I really am trying my best. Is it... is it normal for something like this to mess up your work life so much?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think there's anything that could change your perception of the unevenness on your face, or do you feel like it's something that will always be there for you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / secondary_school_teacher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... wow, that's... I guess that's the million-dollar question, isn't it? 

I really, really wish I could say yes. I'd love to tell you that if I just got enough sleep, or maybe if I found the right lighting, or... or if I just tried harder to be positive, it would go away. I'm usually a very optimistic person, I promise I am. I don't like being stuck in a negative headspace. 

But... honestly? I just don't know. I've tried telling myself it's not a big deal, and I've tried looking in different mirrors, but... it's always just right there. It feels like it's just a part of my face now. 

I hope that doesn't mean I'm a lost cause or anything. I'm really willing to try whatever you suggest! I want to fix this, I really do. I just... I don't know how to change how I see it right now. Is that... is that a bad sign? I hope I'm not being too pessimistic.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... gosh, that's a really good question. I... I want to say yes? 

I mean, if someone could prove to me, like, 100 percent that it was perfectly normal and symmetrical... I'd really like to think I'd believe them. I try to be a reasonable person, you know? I don't want to be someone who just ignores facts. 

But... honestly? Even when people have told me before that it looks fine, it just... it doesn't *feel* fine when I look in the mirror. It's like my brain just won't accept it, even if I want to. 

I'm sorry, I know that probably sounds completely irrational. I know I should just be able to accept it if I knew for sure there was nothing wrong. I feel really silly even admitting that it might still bother me. Is it... is it bad that I'm not sure if I could see it as normal? I really don't want to be difficult about this. I'll try my hardest to see it differently if you think I can!

I see what I see, and it's hard to un-see it, even if I want to. Does that make sense? I don't know how to change how I see it right now. Is that... is that a bad sign? Does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concern about the unevenness on your face is interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Um... well... I guess I'd have to say yes, it is. It's definitely making things harder than they used to be. \n\nI really pride myself on being a good teacher, and I love being involved at the school. But lately... um... I've just been falling behind on things. Like, my lesson planning isn't getting done, and I haven't been as present for mentoring the younger students. Even with coaching the basketball team, I'm just... I'm missing things. \n\nI spend so much time worrying about how I look, or trying to fix it, that my work just isn't getting finished on time anymore. And... I guess it's gotten bad enough that the administrators have noticed. They've started documenting my missed deadlines and some issues with coverage. \n\nI'm so ashamed to admit that, honestly. I've always been the reliable one, you know? And now I'm genuinely terrified I might lose my position if I can't get this under control. I know I shouldn't let something like my face get in the way of my career, and I'm really trying to push past it, but it's just so overwhelming. \n\nI hope you don't think less of me for letting it affect my job. I really am trying my best. Is it... is it normal for something like this to mess up your work life so much?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could change your perception of the unevenness on your face, or do you feel like it's something that will always be there for you?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, that's... I guess that's the million-dollar question, isn't it? \n\nI really, really wish I could say yes. I'd love to tell you that if I just got enough sleep, or maybe if I found the right lighting, or... or if I just tried harder to be positive, it would go away. I'm usually a very optimistic person, I promise I am. I don't like being stuck in a negative headspace. \n\nBut... honestly? I just don't know. I've tried telling myself it's not a big deal, and I've tried looking in different mirrors, but... it's always just right there. I'll try my hardest to see it differently if you think I can!\n\nDoctor: Do you think that even if you received professional confirmation, such as from a doctor or a plastic surgeon, that there is nothing physically wrong with your face, your perception of its unevenness would still remain the same?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, that's... that's really similar to what you just asked, isn't it? I just want to be a good teacher, and it's hard when I feel like this huge flaw is distracting everyone. And the students, well, they haven't said it directly to me, anyway. I'm really trying to be as honest as I can, I just don't want you to think I'm wasting your time if no one else has pointed it out. Um... well, like I think I mentioned just a little bit ago, the thought has definitely crossed my mind... but, no, I haven't actually looked into it seriously or tried to get a real evaluation. I'm sorry if I made it sound earlier like I was actually taking steps to do that. 

I... I think I'm just too embarrassed to actually pick up the phone and make an appointment. I really don't want to waste a busy surgeon's time, you know? What if I go in there and they think I'm just being totally vain, or... or they get annoyed with me because they can't see what I'm talking about? 

Being a high school teacher, I have to stand up in front of teenagers all day, and I already feel so self-conscious about how they see me. The idea of sitting under those bright medical lights and having a professional scrutinize my face... it just feels terrifying. I'd be so ashamed if they thought I was crazy for even being there. 

I hope that makes sense. Is it... is it okay that I haven't tried to see anyone for it yet? I really don't want you to think I'm not trying to get better. I promise I'll look into it if you think it's the right step for me to take.

So even if everyone was being incredibly kind and telling me I looked normal, I think a part of me would just assume they were... well, just being nice. Trying to make me feel better, you know? Because people are good like that. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound stubborn or... or like I wouldn't trust people. I really do value other people's opinions! It's just... I see what I see, and it's hard to un-see it, even if I want to. Does that make sense? I hope that's not a frustrating answer.\n\nDoctor: Has the specific feature of your face that you're concerned about, the unevenness, changed at all since you first started noticing it?\n\nPatient: Um... well, no, I don't think it's actually changed. I mean, physically, it's... it's the same. \n\nI know I've been talking about it a lot, and I really don't want to give the impression that it's, like, growing or mutating or anything crazy like that. I'm not delusional, I promise! It's just... the unevenness has always been there, I guess, but I just didn't really focus on it until I had to watch myself teach on those videos. \n\nSo the feature itself hasn't changed... maybe just how much I notice it? I hope that's an okay answer. I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right information so I'm not wasting your time. Does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concern about the unevenness on your face is interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Um... well... I guess I'd have to say yes, it is. It's definitely making things harder than they used to be. \n\nI really pride myself on being a good teacher, and I love being involved at the school. But lately... um... I've just been falling behind on things. Like, my lesson planning isn't getting done, and I haven't been as present for mentoring the younger students. Even with coaching the basketball team, I'm just... I'm missing things. \n\nI spend so much time worrying about how I look, or trying to fix it, that my work just isn't getting finished on time anymore. And... I guess it's gotten bad enough that the administrators have noticed. They've started documenting my missed deadlines and some issues with coverage. \n\nI'm so ashamed to admit that, honestly. I've always been the reliable one, you know? And now I'm genuinely terrified I might lose my position if I can't get this under control. I know I shouldn't let something like my face get in the way of my career, and I'm really trying to push past it, but it's just so overwhelming. \n\nI hope you don't think less of me for letting it affect my job. I really am trying my best. Is it... is it normal for something like this to mess up your work life so much?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could change your perception of the unevenness on your face, or do you feel like it's something that will always be there for you?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, that's... I guess that's the million-dollar question, isn't it? \n\nI really, really wish I could say yes. I'm usually a very optimistic person, I promise I am. I don't like being stuck in a negative headspace. \n\nBut... honestly? I just don't know. I've tried telling myself it's not a big deal, and I've tried looking in different mirrors, but... it's always just right there. It feels like it's just a part of my face now. \n\nI hope that doesn't mean I'm a lost cause or anything. I'm really willing to try whatever you suggest! I want to fix this, I really do. I just... I don't know how to change how I see it right now. Is that... is that a bad sign? I hope I'm not being too pessimistic.\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of the unevenness on your face ever vary in intensity or does it remain constant?\n\nPatient: Um... I guess I'm not totally sure how to answer that one. \n\nI mean, it feels like it's always there, but... maybe it's not always exactly the same? Like, I guess there are moments where I'm distracted enough teaching a class that I'm not actively thinking about it, so maybe it's less intense then? But as soon as I catch my reflection or think about the videos, it's just... right back to being really overwhelming. \n\nI haven't really tracked it, to be honest. I'm sorry, I know I should probably pay better attention to things like that so I can give you better answers. I just... I try so hard to push it out of my mind that I don't always notice if it's fluctuating. \n\nIs it... is it supposed to change? I can try to start keeping a log of it if that would be helpful for you! I really want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to figure this out. Did that make sense, or did I totally miss the point of your question?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face could ever look normal to you, even if you knew for certain that it was perfectly symmetrical and there was nothing physically wrong with it?\n\nPatient: Um... gosh, that's a really good question. I... I want to say yes? \n\nI mean, if someone could prove to me, like, 100 percent that it was perfectly normal and symmetrical... I'd really like to think I'd believe them. I try to be a reasonable person, you know? I don't want to be someone who just ignores facts. \n\nBut... honestly? Even when people have told me before that it looks fine, it just... it doesn't *feel* fine when I look in the mirror. It's like my brain just won't accept it, even if I want to. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that probably sounds completely irrational. I know I should just be able to accept it if I knew for sure there was nothing wrong. I feel really silly even admitting that it might still bother me. I just want to be a good teacher, and it's hard when I feel like this huge flaw is distracting everyone. I hope that doesn't make me sound terrible... is that the kind of information you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: Do you think that others actually notice this unevenness on your face, or is it just your own perception?\n\nPatient: Um... well, I know I just sort of mentioned this a second ago, but... because I just can't stop seeing how crooked and swollen it looks on those lesson videos, I... I really do believe they notice it. \n\nI don't want to sound paranoid or anything, I promise I'm usually a very rational person. I know you might think it's just my own perception, and I really wish it was... but yes, I feel like the students, the parents, the staff... they must all be seeing it and staring at it instead of listening to my lessons. I know they're probably too polite to point it out to my face, but it just feels so obvious to me that they're looking right at it. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm crazy or delusional for feeling that way... I really try to be objective about things. Does that... does that answer your question okay?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever received any feedback from others about your appearance that confirms your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Um... no. No, no one has actually come right out and said anything to me about it. \n\nI... I know that probably makes it sound like it's all just in my head, and I really don't want you to think I'm making things up or exaggerating. I promise I'm not. I just figure... well, people are usually polite, you know? Especially in a professional environment. The other teachers aren't going to just walk up and tell a colleague their face looks crooked. And the students, well, they haven't said it directly to me, anyway. I'm sure they probably just talk about it behind my back instead of being rude to my face. \n\nBut no, no direct comments or feedback confirming it. I hope... I hope that makes sense? I'm really trying to be as honest as I can, I just don't want you to think I'm wasting your time if no one else has pointed it out. Is that an okay answer?\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your face been affecting your relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: Um... well, no, not really. I mean, my family and friends are all wonderful, really supportive people. I... I try really hard not to let this bother them or bring them down. \n\nI know it can be exhausting to be around someone who's always worrying about how they look, and I really don't want to be a burden to anyone. So I usually just keep it to myself when I'm around them. I don't want to seem superficial or like I'm fishing for compliments or anything like that. So... no, things are fine with my family and friends. I just try to put on a brave face, you know? \n\nI hope that's alright... I just really want to be a good friend and son, and not let my own silly insecurities ruin things for the people I care about. Does that... is that what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the unevenness on the side of your face something that you think about all the time, or are there times when you don't think about it at all?\n\nPatient: Um... well... I guess I think about it a lot. Especially lately. \n\nI know I've mentioned the lesson videos a couple of times already, and I really hope I don't sound like a broken record... I just want to make sure I'm explaining myself right. But yes, those videos are really what started it. I just can't stop seeing how crooked and swollen that side of my face looks on the screen. And once I notice it, it's just... it's stuck in my head. \n\nI try to push it away, I really do. I want to focus on my teaching, because I love my job and I want to do well. But it just keeps creeping back in, and then I'm right back to feeling like all the students and parents and staff are just staring at it instead of listening to a word I'm saying. So... I guess it's almost always on my mind, even when I'm trying my hardest to ignore it. \n\nI know that probably sounds a bit obsessive, and I really don't want you to think I'm completely losing it over something small. I'm usually very grounded, I promise. It's just... it's hard to turn off once it's in my head. Is that... does that make sense? Am I answering your question okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look normal to you if everyone around you told you it looked fine?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, that's... that's a really good question. I, um... \n\nI want to say yes? I really want to say that if everyone told me it was fine, I'd just believe them and move on. I mean, I'd certainly appreciate them saying it, and I'd probably smile and thank them so I wouldn't seem ungrateful or... or argumentative. I hate being difficult. \n\nBut... if I'm being completely honest—and I really am trying to be as honest as I can with you—I don't know if it would actually change what I see in the mirror. I mean, physically, it's... it's the same. \n\nI know I've been talking about it a lot, and I really don't want to give the impression that it's, like, growing or mutating or anything crazy like that. I hope that's an okay answer. I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right information so I'm not wasting your time. Does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concern about the unevenness on your face is interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Um... well... I'm missing things. \n\nI spend so much time worrying about how I look, or trying to fix it, that my work just isn't getting finished on time anymore. And... I've always been the reliable one, you know? And now I'm genuinely terrified I might lose my position if I can't get this under control. I know I shouldn't let something like my face get in the way of my career, and I'm really trying to push past it, but it's just so overwhelming. \n\nI hope you don't think less of me for letting it affect my job. I really am trying my best. Is it... is it normal for something like this to mess up your work life so much?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could change your perception of the unevenness on your face, or do you feel like it's something that will always be there for you?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, that's... I guess that's the million-dollar question, isn't it? \n\nI really, really wish I could say yes. I'd love to tell you that if I just got enough sleep, or maybe if I found the right lighting, or... or if I just tried harder to be positive, it would go away. I'm usually a very optimistic person, I promise I am. I don't like being stuck in a negative headspace. \n\nBut... honestly? I just don't know. I've tried telling myself it's not a big deal, and I've tried looking in different mirrors, but... it's always just right there. It feels like it's just a part of my face now. \n\nI hope that doesn't mean I'm a lost cause or anything. I'm really willing to try whatever you suggest! I want to fix this, I really do. I just... I don't know how to change how I see it right now. Is that... is that a bad sign? I try so hard to push it out of my mind that I don't always notice if it's fluctuating. \n\nIs it... is it supposed to change? I can try to start keeping a log of it if that would be helpful for you! I really want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to figure this out. Did that make sense, or did I totally miss the point of your question?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face could ever look normal to you, even if you knew for certain that it was perfectly symmetrical and there was nothing physically wrong with it?\n\nPatient: Um... gosh, that's a really good question. I... I want to say yes? \n\nI mean, if someone could prove to me, like, 100 percent that it was perfectly normal and symmetrical... I'd really like to think I'd believe them. I try to be a reasonable person, you know? I don't want to be someone who just ignores facts. \n\nBut... honestly? Even when people have told me before that it looks fine, it just... it doesn't *feel* fine when I look in the mirror. It's like my brain just won't accept it, even if I want to. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that probably sounds completely irrational. I know I should just be able to accept it if I knew for sure there was nothing wrong. I feel really silly even admitting that it might still bother me. Is it... is it bad that I'm not sure if I could see it as normal? I really don't want to be difficult about this. I'll try my hardest to see it differently if you think I can!\n\nDoctor: Do you think that even if you received professional confirmation, such as from a doctor or a plastic surgeon, that there is nothing physically wrong with your face, your perception of its unevenness would still remain the same?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, that's... that's really similar to what you just asked, isn't it? I'm sorry, I hope my last answer wasn't too confusing. \n\nI guess... if a professional, like a surgeon, told me it was fine... I'd really, really want to believe them. I respect doctors so much, and I know they're the experts. I would never want to argue with someone who has spent years studying this stuff! \n\nBut... I don't know. I've actually thought about going to a plastic surgeon, just to see what they would say, but I've been too scared they'd either confirm how bad it is, or... or tell me I'm crazy for seeing it. If they did say it was perfectly fine, I'd probably just worry that they were being nice, or that they just couldn't see what I see in the mirror every single day. \n\nI know that's probably not the right answer. I should just trust the professionals, right? I'm really sorry, I know it sounds like I'm just being stubborn. I don't want to be one of those patients who refuses to listen. I'll try my hardest to see it differently if you think I can!\n\nDoctor: Do you think that even if you received professional confirmation, such as from a doctor or a plastic surgeon, that there is nothing physically wrong with your face, your perception of its unevenness would still remain the same?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, that's... that's really similar to what you just asked, isn't it? I'm sorry, I hope my last answer wasn't too confusing. \n\nI guess... if a professional, like a surgeon, told me it was fine... I'd really, really want to believe them. I respect doctors so much, and I know they're the experts. I would never want to argue with someone who has spent years studying this stuff! \n\nBut... I don't know. I've actually thought about going to a plastic surgeon, just to see what they would say, but I've been too scared they'd either confirm how bad it is, or... or tell me I'm crazy for seeing it. If they did say it was perfectly fine, I'd probably just worry that they were being nice, or that they just couldn't see what I see in the mirror every single day. \n\nI know that's probably not the right answer. I should just trust the professionals, right? I'm really sorry, I know it sounds like I'm just being stubborn. I don't want to be one of those patients who refuses to listen. I'd really try to accept what they said, I promise I would. Is it... is it normal to feel this unsure about it? I hope I'm not frustrating you with these answers.\n\nDoctor: If a plastic surgeon told you that your face is perfectly symmetrical and there's nothing physically wrong with it, would that completely alleviate your concern about its unevenness?\n\nPatient: Um... oh gosh, I feel like I'm just repeating myself, and I'm really sorry if I'm not giving you the answer you're looking for. \n\nI really, really want to say yes. Like, if a surgeon told me that, I would want that to be the end of it, you know? I would want to just walk out of their office and never worry about it again. That would be the dream. \n\nBut... I honestly don't know if it would completely take the concern away. I feel like... I feel like I'd still go home and look in the mirror and just... see it. Even if I kept repeating to myself what the doctor said. It's like my eyes and my brain are just disconnected from reality sometimes. \n\nI'm so sorry, I know I'm probably sounding like a broken record, and I really don't mean to be difficult. I know I should just be able to say, \"Yes, a doctor's word would fix it,\" and I feel awful that I can't promise that. I'm really trying to be honest with you so you can help me. Am I... am I making any sense at all? I hope I'm not wasting your time with all this back-and-forth.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered seeking a professional medical or surgical evaluation to assess the physical appearance of your face and determine if there is an actual physical issue present?\n\nPatient: Um... well, like I think I mentioned just a little bit ago, the thought has definitely crossed my mind... but, no, I haven't actually looked into it seriously or tried to get a real evaluation. I'm sorry if I made it sound earlier like I was actually taking steps to do that. \n\nI... I think I'm just too embarrassed to actually pick up the phone and make an appointment. I really don't want to waste a busy surgeon's time, you know? What if I go in there and they think I'm just being totally vain, or... or they get annoyed with me because they can't see what I'm talking about? \n\nBeing a high school teacher, I have to stand up in front of teenagers all day, and I already feel so self-conscious about how they see me. The idea of sitting under those bright medical lights and having a professional scrutinize my face... it just feels terrifying. I'd be so ashamed if they thought I was crazy for even being there. \n\nI hope that makes sense. Is it... is it okay that I haven't tried to see anyone for it yet? I really don't want you to think I'm not trying to get better. I promise I'll look into it if you think it's the right step for me to take.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of the unevenness on your face could be a product of your own mind, rather than an actual physical flaw?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, that's... that's a really good question. I mean, I guess I've sort of wondered about that sometimes? Like, maybe I'm just focusing too much on it, and it's not as glaring to everyone else as it is to me. \n\nBut when I look in the mirror, it just... it feels so real. The unevenness is right there, and I can't unsee it. So it's hard to just say it's all in my head, you know? But I also know I'm not a doctor, and I definitely don't want to sound like I'm arguing with you or questioning your expertise. \n\nIf you think it's possible that it's just my mind playing tricks on me, then... then maybe it is? I really want to be open to whatever you think is going on. I'm just trying to make sense of why it feels so obvious to me. Does that... does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too defensive. I really do value your perspective on this.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the unevenness you see in your face isn't actually there, and it's just your mind's interpretation?\n\nPatient: Um... I'm so sorry, I know you kind of just asked me something really similar, and I feel awful if I didn't give you a clear enough answer a second ago. I really don't mean to be confusing or difficult. \n\nI... I honestly just don't know for sure. I want to say yes, absolutely, it's just my mind's interpretation—because I know that's probably what you're trying to help me see, and I really do trust your judgment. But I just don't have a solid answer to give you. When I look in the mirror, my eyes are telling me the unevenness is physically right there, so it's just really hard to separate what I'm seeing from what might actually be real. \n\nI'm sorry, I feel like I'm just going in circles and probably frustrating you. Is it... is it okay that I'm not totally sure? I really want to give you the right answer and be helpful, I'm just... I'm really confused by my own reflection. I hope you're not getting annoyed with me."
